# Plan: Objective Dimension-Based Scoring System

## Context

The current scoring (`strict_score = fixed_weight / total_weight * 100`) is subjective — it measures what percentage of *detected findings* have been addressed, not how healthy the codebase actually is. A codebase with 2 findings (1 fixed) scores 50%, but one with 1000 findings (500 fixed) also scores 50%. The score is about cleanup effort, not codebase health.

**The fix**: each detector tracks the **total universe of things it checked** (potentials) alongside what failed. Score = `things_that_passed / things_checked * 100`. An objective health metric.

**Dynamic and self-healing**: every full scan re-enumerates the entire universe from scratch. New files → potentials increase. Deleted files → potentials decrease, old findings auto-resolve. New detectors → new potentials appear. Removed detectors → potentials disappear, old findings auto-resolve. The state file is always the primary source of truth, always reflecting the current codebase.

---

## Architecture: Dimensions

Group detectors into **dimensions** — coherent aspects of code quality that a human cares about. Each dimension has a name, a tier, and maps to finding detector names.

```python
@dataclass
class Dimension:
    name: str
    tier: int
    detectors: list[str]  # finding detector names (first arg to make_finding)

DIMENSIONS = [
    Dimension("Import hygiene",      1, ["unused"]),
    Dimension("Debug cleanliness",   1, ["logs"]),
    Dimension("API surface",         2, ["exports", "deprecated"]),
    Dimension("File health",         3, ["structural"]),
    Dimension("Component design",    3, ["props"]),
    Dimension("Coupling",            3, ["single_use", "coupling"]),
    Dimension("Organization",        3, ["orphaned", "flat_dirs", "naming"]),
    Dimension("Code quality",        3, ["smells", "react"]),
    Dimension("Duplication",         3, ["dupes"]),
    Dimension("Pattern consistency", 3, ["patterns"]),
    Dimension("Dependency health",   4, ["cycles"]),
]
```

**11 dimensions.** Each maps to a real question: "Is my code well-organized? Are my abstractions properly scoped? Do I have unnecessary coupling?"

### Detector name audit (critical for correctness)

These are the **actual finding detector names** from `make_finding()` calls in the codebase:

| Finding detector | Created by | Language |
|-----------------|------------|---------|
| `unused` | ruff/tsc unused detection | both |
| `logs` | debug log detection | TS only |
| `exports` | dead export detection | TS only |
| `deprecated` | deprecated symbol detection | TS only |
| `structural` | large + complexity + gods + concerns merged per-file | both |
| `props` | prop bloat + passthrough | both |
| `single_use` | single-use abstraction detection | both |
| `coupling` | boundary violations + cross-tool imports | TS only |
| `orphaned` | zero-importer file detection | both |
| `cycles` | import cycle detection | both |
| `smells` | code smell patterns | both |
| `react` | React state sync anti-patterns | TS only |
| `dupes` | duplicate function detection | both |
| `patterns` | pattern consistency anomalies | TS only |
| `naming` | file naming inconsistencies | TS only |

**Required fix**: flat_dirs currently creates findings with `detector="structural"`. Change to `detector="flat_dirs"` so Organization dimension can track it separately. Minor state compat break (old findings auto-resolve, new ones created — normal scan behavior).

### What about "gods" and "concerns"?

These are **not separate finding detector names**. They produce signals that merge into per-file `"structural"` findings via `merge_structural_signals()`. A file that is large + complex + has a god class = one `"structural"` finding. The "structural" potential = total files checked. This is correct — we're asking "what fraction of files have any structural issue?"

---

## Score Computation

```
Per detector:
  failures = findings with status in failure_set
  weighted_failures = sum(CONFIDENCE_WEIGHTS[f.confidence] for f in failures)
  pass_rate = (potential - weighted_failures) / potential
  (confidence weights: high=1.0, medium=0.7, low=0.3)

Per dimension:
  Only include detectors that have potentials > 0
  dimension_score = average(detector pass_rates within dimension)

Overall:
  objective_score = weighted_average(dimension_scores, weights=tier_weights)
  (tier weights: T1=1, T2=2, T3=3, T4=4)
```

**Two scores:**
- **Health** (lenient): only `open` = failures (wontfix = accepted debt, doesn't penalize)
- **Strict**: `open` + `wontfix` = failures (real debt in the code)

`false_positive`, `fixed`, `auto_resolved` never count as failures.

**Edge cases:**
- Potential = 0 for a detector → 100% pass rate (nothing to check, nothing failed)
- Dimension with no active detectors (all potentials = 0 or missing) → excluded from overall score
- Weighted failures > potential (shouldn't happen, but guard) → clamp pass_rate to 0%
- Old state without potentials → fall back to current scoring. First scan after upgrade populates potentials.

---

## What Each Detector Reports as Potential

| Detector | Potential = | How counted |
|----------|------------|-------------|
| `unused` | total import/var statements | ruff/tsc already counts all; add a quick count pass or use total from tool output |
| `logs` | total files scanned | `len(file_finder(path))` |
| `exports` | total named exports | Count during detection (already iterates all) |
| `deprecated` | total `@deprecated` annotations | Count during detection |
| `structural` | total source files | `len(file_finder(path))` — covers large + complexity + gods + concerns |
| `props` | total prop interfaces / components | Already extracted during detection |
| `single_use` | total candidate files | Files in shared/lib dirs with exactly 1 importer — already filtered |
| `coupling` | total boundary-crossing import edges | Already enumerated during detection |
| `orphaned` | total files in dep graph | Already known from graph |
| `flat_dirs` | total directories | `len(set(Path(f).parent for f in files))` |
| `cycles` | total dependency edges | `sum(len(v.get("imports", [])) for v in graph.values())` |
| `smells` | total files scanned | `len(file_finder(path))` |
| `react` | total useEffect calls | Quick regex count during detection |
| `dupes` | total function pairs checked | `n*(n-1)/2` where n = extracted functions |
| `patterns` | total areas | Already counted during detection |
| `naming` | total directories | Already counted during detection |

Most are trivially available — the detector already iterates the universe.

---

## Return Type Changes

**Detectors**: `list[dict]` → `tuple[list[dict], int]`
```python
# Before
def detect_large_files(path, file_finder, threshold=500):
    files = file_finder(path)
    return [entry for f in files if too_large(f)]

# After
def detect_large_files(path, file_finder, threshold=500):
    files = file_finder(path)
    entries = [entry for f in files if too_large(f)]
    return entries, len(files)
```

**Phase runners**: `list[dict]` → `tuple[list[dict], dict[str, int]]`
```python
def _phase_structural(path, lang):
    large_entries, large_pot = detect_large_files(...)
    complexity_entries, _ = detect_complexity(...)  # shares file universe with large
    god_entries, god_pot = detect_gods(...)
    flat_entries, flat_pot = detect_flat_dirs(...)
    # ... merge into findings as before ...
    potentials = {
        "structural": large_pot,   # large + complexity + gods + concerns → per-file
        "flat_dirs": flat_pot,
    }
    return findings, potentials
```

**`generate_findings`**: `list[dict]` → `tuple[list[dict], dict[str, int]]`

**Backward compat**: phase runners that haven't been updated return just `list[dict]`. `generate_findings` checks `isinstance(result, tuple)` and handles both.

## State Storage

```json
{
  "version": 2,
  "potentials": {
    "typescript": { "unused": 3247, "logs": 412, "exports": 1893, "structural": 412, ... },
    "python": { "unused": 500, "structural": 50, ... }
  },
  "codebase_metrics": {
    "typescript": { "total_files": 412, "total_loc": 87432, "total_directories": 52 },
    "python": { "total_files": 50, "total_loc": 8200, "total_directories": 12 }
  },
  "objective_score": 94.2,
  "objective_strict": 91.8,
  "dimension_scores": {
    "Import hygiene": { "score": 98.7, "strict": 97.2, "checks": 3747, "issues": 42 },
    "Coupling": { "score": 90.8, "strict": 88.4, "checks": 87, "issues": 8 }
  },
  "score": 85.0,
  "strict_score": 72.0,
  "findings": { ... }
}
```

**Per-language potentials**: stored under `potentials[lang_name]`. When scanning Python, only `potentials["python"]` is replaced — TypeScript potentials persist from last TS scan. For overall scoring, potentials are **summed across languages** per detector (e.g., `unused = ts.unused + py.unused`).

**Each full scan replaces ALL potentials for that language.** This means:
- New detector added → its potential appears automatically
- Detector removed → its potential disappears
- Files added/deleted → all potentials adjust

**Old `score`/`strict_score` kept** for backward compat — they become "progress" scores (what % addressed). Objective scores are the "health" scores.

---

## Dynamic Self-Healing Behavior

### New files added → next scan:
- `file_finder` discovers new files → potentials increase
- New findings may appear → failures increase
- Score adjusts: if new files are clean, score stays same or rises slightly. If new files have issues, score drops.
- **Correct**: adding bad code lowers the health score.

### Files deleted → next scan:
- `file_finder` no longer sees them → potentials decrease
- Old findings disappear from scan results → auto-resolve
- Score adjusts naturally upward (fewer issues, fewer things to check).

### Detector added (new code) → next scan:
- New detector runs → new potentials appear
- If it finds 0 issues → dimension pass rate improves (new clean dimension)
- If it finds issues → score may drop → **correct behavior**

### Detector removed (code deleted) → next scan:
- Detector doesn't run → its potential doesn't appear in the replacement dict
- Old findings from that detector don't appear in current scan → auto-resolve
- Dimension that loses all its detectors → excluded from scoring

### Codebase grows/shrinks:
- `codebase_metrics` updated on each full scan
- Potentials scale naturally with codebase size
- A 100-file project with 2 structural issues = same pass rate as a 1000-file project with 20 structural issues = 98% File health

### Language-scoped scan (`--lang python`):
- Only Python potentials update
- TS potentials persist from last TS scan
- Overall score uses both sets of potentials
- Staleness warning if TS data is old

### Path-scoped scan (`--path src/tools/video`):
- **Does NOT update potentials** (artificially small universe)
- Findings still update (new/resolved/reopened)
- Potentials and objective score come from last full scan
- This prevents a subdirectory scan from making the score look artificially good

## Implementation Steps

### Step 1: Create `scoring.py` (~130 lines)

**File**: `desloppify/scoring.py` — NEW

```python
@dataclass
class Dimension:
    name: str
    tier: int
    detectors: list[str]

DIMENSIONS = [...]  # 11 dimensions as above

TIER_WEIGHTS = {1: 1, 2: 2, 3: 3, 4: 4}
CONFIDENCE_WEIGHTS = {"high": 1.0, "medium": 0.7, "low": 0.3}

def merge_potentials(potentials_by_lang: dict) -> dict[str, int]:
    """Sum potentials across languages per detector."""

def compute_dimension_scores(findings: dict, potentials: dict[str, int],
                              *, strict: bool = False) -> dict[str, dict]:
    """Compute per-dimension scores from findings and potentials."""

def compute_objective_score(dimension_scores: dict) -> float:
    """Tier-weighted average of dimension scores."""

def _detector_pass_rate(detector: str, findings: dict, potential: int,
                        *, strict: bool = False) -> float:
    """Pass rate for one detector. Handles zero-potential (returns 1.0)."""

def compute_score_impact(dimension_scores: dict, detector: str,
                         issues_to_fix: int, potential: int) -> float:
    """Estimate score improvement from fixing N issues in a detector."""
```

### Step 4: Update phase runners to collect potentials (~8 functions)

**Files**: `lang/python/__init__.py`, `lang/typescript/__init__.py`, `lang/base.py`

Each phase runner now collects potentials from its sub-detectors.

Example — TS `_phase_structural`:
```python
def _phase_structural(path, lang):
    large_entries, file_count = detect_large_files(...)
    complexity_entries, _ = detect_complexity(...)  # same file universe
    god_entries, god_count = detect_gods(...)
    concern_entries, _ = detect_mixed_concerns(...)  # same file universe
    flat_entries, dir_count = detect_flat_dirs(...)
    prop_entries, prop_count = detect_prop_interface_bloat(...)
    pt_entries, pt_count = detect_passthrough_components(...)
    # ... merge structural signals, build findings as before ...
    potentials = {
        "structural": file_count,     # large + complexity + gods + concerns → per-file
        "flat_dirs": dir_count,
        "props": max(prop_count, pt_count),  # prop bloat + passthrough share component universe
    }
    return findings, potentials
```

Update shared phase helpers in `lang/base.py`:
- `phase_dupes` → `return findings, {"dupes": total_pairs}`
- Other `make_*_findings` helpers: accept optional potential param, pass through

### Step 5: Update `generate_findings` in `plan.py`

**File**: `desloppify/plan.py`

```python
def _generate_findings_from_lang(path, lang, *, include_slow=True):
    findings = []
    all_potentials = {}
    for phase in phases:
        result = phase.run(path, lang)
        if isinstance(result, tuple):
            phase_findings, phase_potentials = result
            all_potentials.update(phase_potentials)
        else:
            phase_findings = result  # backward compat
        findings.extend(phase_findings)
    # ... stamp language ...
    return findings, all_potentials
```

Also collect codebase metrics here (total files, LOC, directories) from `file_finder`.

### Step 6: Update `state.py` — store potentials and compute objective scores

**File**: `desloppify/state.py`

- `merge_scan` gains `potentials: dict[str, int] | None = None` and `codebase_metrics: dict | None = None`
- On full scan (potentials provided):
  ```python
  if potentials is not None:
      state.setdefault("potentials", {})[lang] = potentials
      state.setdefault("codebase_metrics", {})[lang] = codebase_metrics
  ```
- `_recompute_stats` now also calls scoring:
  ```python
  from .scoring import merge_potentials, compute_dimension_scores, compute_objective_score
  all_potentials = merge_potentials(state.get("potentials", {}))
  if all_potentials:  # have objective data
      dim_scores = compute_dimension_scores(state["findings"], all_potentials)
      strict_dim_scores = compute_dimension_scores(state["findings"], all_potentials, strict=True)
      state["dimension_scores"] = {
          name: {"score": dim_scores[name], "strict": strict_dim_scores[name],
                 "checks": ..., "issues": ...}
          for name in dim_scores
      }
      state["objective_score"] = compute_objective_score(dim_scores)
      state["objective_strict"] = compute_objective_score(strict_dim_scores)
  ```
- **Keep old `score`/`strict_score`** for backward compat

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   The user asked to implement a detailed plan for an "Objective Dimension-Based Scoring System" for the `desloppify` code quality tool. The current scoring measures what percentage of detected findings have been addressed (subjective), while the new system measures what fraction of total things checked passed (objective health metric). The plan involves:
   - Creating a `scoring.py` module with dimension definitions and score computation
   - Making each detector return `(entries, potential_count)` tuples
   - Propagating potentials through phase runners → `generate_findings` → `merge_scan` → state
   - Updating display commands (scan, status, next, plan) to show dimension-based health scores
   - Supporting per-language potentials, dynamic self-healing (new/deleted files adjust scores)
   
   Additionally, the user sent: "Once you're done, test it on the parent code-base (directory up, typescript) and this codebase (.py)"

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - **Dimensions**: 11 coherent aspects of code quality (Import hygiene, Debug cleanliness, API surface, File health, Component design, Coupling, Organization, Code quality, Duplication, Pattern consistency, Dependency health), each mapping to specific detector names
   - **Potentials**: The total universe of things a detector checked (e.g., total files, total exports, total edges)
   - **Pass rate**: `(potential - weighted_failures) / potential * 100` per detector, with confidence weights (high=1.0, medium=0.7, low=0.3)
   - **Tier-weighted scoring**: Dimensions have tiers (T1-T4), weighted {1:1, 2:2, 3:3, 4:4}
   - **Two scores**: Health (only `open` = failures) and Strict (`open` + `wontfix` = failures)
   - **Per-language potentials**: Stored under `potentials[lang_name]`, summed across languages for scoring
   - **Path-scoped scans**: Do NOT update potentials (to avoid artificially small universe)
   - **Detector return type change**: `list[dict]` → `tuple[list[dict], int]`
   - **Phase runner return type change**: `list[dict]` → `tuple[list[dict], dict[str, int]]`
   - Base path for all desloppify files: `/user_c042661f/.pyenv/versions/3.11.11/lib/python3.11/site-packages/desloppify/`

Good. Let me do a thorough sense-check of the results before pushing.

## Sense-check: TypeScript scan (95.2/100)

**What's right:**

| Dimension | Score | Findings/Potential | Verification |
|-----------|-------|-------------------|-------------|
| Import hygiene | 99.5% | 5 / 958 | 5 unused from 958 files. Correct. |
| Debug cleanliness | 99.4% | 6 / 958 | 6 log groups from 958 files. Correct. |
| API surface | 96.7% | 4 / 1505 | exports: 99.9% (1/1459), deprecated: 93.5% (3/46), avg=96.7%. Correct — small deprecated universe pulls down the average. |
| File health | 92.5% | 94 / 958 | 94 structural findings, some medium confidence (0.7 weight) so weighted <94. Correct. |
| Component design | 84.3% | 97 / 447 | 97 prop bloat+passthrough from 447 interfaces. Most medium confidence. Correct. |
| Coupling | 97.8% | 36 / 3200 | single_use: 95.8% (29/489, medium), coupling: 99.7% (7/2711, high), avg=97.8%. Correct. |
| Organization | 98.9% | 8 / 1259 | orphaned: 100%, flat_dirs: 96.6% (8/165, medium), naming: 100%, avg=98.9%. Correct. |
| Code quality | 95.8% | 106 / 1180 | smells+react, mixed confidence. Consistent. |
| Pattern consistency | 91.7% | 5 / 42 | 5 anomalies from 42 areas. Consistent. |
| Dependency health | 99.9% | 4 / 3942 | 4 import cycles from 3942 edges. Correct. |

**Duplication missing** — skipped with `--skip-slow`. Correctly excluded (no potentials = dimension omitted).

## Sense-check: Python scan (79.7/100)

| Dimension | Score | Findings/Potential | Issue? |
|-----------|-------|-------------------|--------|
| Import hygiene | 26.7% | 11 / 15 | **Concern**: 11 findings from 15 files. Each file can have multiple unused imports. If a file has 3 unused imports = 3 findings. This could theoretically push weighted_failures > potential. Currently 11 < 15 so OK, but fragile. |
| File health | 81.3% | 4 / 15 | Fine — 4 structural issues from 15 files. |
| Coupling | 100% | 0 / 4 | Correct — no single_use or coupling violations. |
| Organization | 90.7% | 4 / 19 | orphaned (4/15) + flat_dirs (0/4), weighted correctly. |
| Code quality | 37.3% | 14 / 15 | **Same concern** as unused: 14 smell findings from 15 files. Multiple smells per file possible. |
| Dependency health | 100% | 0 / 4 | Correct. |

## Known design trade-off

**Potential = total files** for `unused` and `smells` detectors. The plan called for "total import statements" and "total smell-checkable patterns" respectively, but the detectors return total files. This means:
- Works well for large codebases (TS: 5/958 = 99.5%, clearly healthy)
- Can overweight findings for small codebases (Python: 11/15 = 26.7%, because one file with 3 unused imports = 3 findings from 1 potential)

This is a **v2 improvement** — changing to per-finding potentials requires counting total imports/statements in each detector, which is extra work for marginal benefit on larger codebases.


Good — `detect_orphaned_files` iterates the graph, which is now filtered by `finalize_graph`. The `dynamic_import_finder` calls `build_dynamic_import_targets` which also uses unfiltered grep, but those targets are just import specifier strings (like `./foo/bar`), not file paths that would match exclusion patterns. Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   The user asked to implement a detailed "Objective Dimension-Based Scoring System" for the `desloppify` code quality tool (plan was pre-written in `/user_c042661f/.claude/plans/snug-whistling-crescent.md`). The system replaces subjective progress scoring (% of findings addressed) with objective health scoring (% of checked items that are clean). Each detector returns `(entries, potential_count)` tuples, 11 dimensions group detectors into coherent quality aspects, and scores are tier-weighted.

   Additional explicit user requests during the session:
   - "Once you're done, test it on the parent code-base (directory up, typescript) and this codebase (.py)"
   - "ONce you're happy with this, push to github and then solve the bug report we have in issues (add a task for this to the end of your list!)"
   - "Check the details of what's wrong for both too - see if it all makes sense - and what's right! Sense-check it!"
   - "Repo is here but first perform the sense-check: https://github.com/peteromallet/desloppify/"
   - "And then there are multiple issues to address"
   - "make sure to add everything for both react and python" (most recent — referring to new detectors from Issue #3)

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - **Dimensions**: 11 coherent aspects of code quality (Import hygiene, Debug cleanliness, API surface, File health, Component design, Coupling, Organization, Code quality, Duplication, Pattern consistency, Dependency health)
   - **Potentials**: Total universe of things a detector checked (e.g., total files, total exports, total edges)
   - **Pass rate**: `(potential - weighted_failures) / potential * 100` per detector, with confidence weights (high=1.0, medium=0.7, low=0.3)
   - **Tier-weighted scoring**: Dimensions have tiers (T1-T4), weighted {1:1, 2:2, 3:3, 4:4}
   - **Two scores**: Health (only `open` = failures) and Strict (`open` + `wontfix` = failures)
   - **Per-language potentials**: Stored under `potentials[lang_name]`, summed across languages for scoring
   - **Detector return type change**: `list[dict]` → `tuple[list[dict], int]`
   - **Phase runner return type change**: `list[dict]` → `tuple[list[dict], dict[str, int]]`
   - **Graph exclusion filtering**: `finalize_graph()` now removes excluded nodes and cleans import/importer sets
   - **AST-based smell detection**: New detectors using Python's `ast` module for monster_function, dead_function, inline_class, deferred_import
   - Base path for all desloppify files: `/user_c042661f/.pyenv/versions/3.11.11/lib/python3.11/site-packages/desloppify/`
   - Git repo clone at: `/tmp/desloppify/`

3. Files and Code Sections:

4. Errors and fixes:
   - **Issue #2 (Python structural crash)**: `TypeError: list indices must be integers or slices, not str` from iterating over a tuple returned by `detect_large_files`. Already fixed by the dimension scoring commit which properly unpacks `large_entries, file_count = detect_large_files(...)`.
   - **Issue #1 (--exclude not filtering graph-based detectors)**: Root cause was `build_dep_graph` in both Python and TS using raw `subprocess.run` instead of `run_grep()`, so exclusion patterns were never applied to graph construction. Fixed by: (1) replacing `subprocess.run` with `run_grep()` in both dep graph builders, (2) adding exclusion filtering in `finalize_graph()` as a safety net.
   - **Missing `tier` and `detectors` in stored dimension_scores**: The `_recompute_stats` function initially only stored `score`, `strict`, `checks`, `issues` in dimension_scores state. This meant `compute_score_impact` couldn't produce "+X pts" estimates. Fixed by also storing `tier` and `detectors` sub-dict.
   - **Potential = total files for unused/smells**: Identified as a design trade-off where findings-per-file can exceed 1, causing weighted_failures to potentially exceed potential. Acceptable for large codebases (TS: 5/958), more visible for small ones (Python: 11/15). Clamped at 0 via `max(0.0, ...)`.

**Phase 1: Adding TypeScript Smell Detectors**
I read the TS smells file at `/user_c042661f/.pyenv/versions/3.11.11/lib/python3.11/site-packages/desloppify/lang/typescript/detectors/smells.py`

Added 4 new checks to `TS_SMELL_CHECKS`:
- `hardcoded_url` (regex)
- `todo_fixme` (regex)
- `monster_function` (brace-tracking, pattern=None)
- `dead_function` (brace-tracking, pattern=None)

Added helper functions:
- `_track_brace_body()` - shared brace-tracking utility
- `_find_function_start()` - determines if a line starts a named function
- `_detect_monster_functions()` - finds functions >150 LOC
- `_detect_dead_functions()` - finds empty/return-only functions

**False Positive Fix:**
Initial implementation had false positives where `const X = useRef(...)`, `const X = useMemo(...)` were matched as functions because `=>` appeared in type annotations or callback arguments. Fixed by tightening `_find_function_start` to check what follows `=`:

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - **Desloppify**: A Python CLI code quality tool installed at `/user_c042661f/.pyenv/versions/3.11.11/lib/python3.11/site-packages/desloppify/`
   - **Git repo clone**: `/tmp/desloppify/` — all edits go here, then pushed to GitHub
   - **Dimension-based scoring**: 11 dimensions, tier-weighted (T1-T4), confidence-weighted (high=1.0, medium=0.7, low=0.3)
   - **Health vs Strict scores**: Health counts only `open` as failures; Strict counts `open` + `wontfix`
   - **Potentials**: Total universe of things a detector checked; score = (potential - weighted_failures) / potential
   - **Per-language state files**: `.desloppify/state-python.json`, `.desloppify/state-typescript.json`
   - **macOS symlink path bug**: `/tmp` → `/private/tmp` causes `Path.cwd()` vs `Path.resolve()` mismatches, creating duplicate structural findings
   - **Brace-tracking for TS**: Since Python's `ast` module can't parse TypeScript, TS detectors use brace-depth tracking with string-literal awareness
   - **Function detection heuristic for TS**: `_find_function_start()` distinguishes `const foo = () => {` (function) from `const foo = useMemo(() => {` (function call) by checking what follows `=`

3. Files and Code Sections:

   - **`/tmp/desloppify/desloppify/lang/typescript/detectors/smells.py`** (installed copy also modified)
     - Added 4 new smell checks to `TS_SMELL_CHECKS`: `hardcoded_url`, `todo_fixme`, `monster_function`, `dead_function`
     - Added helper functions: `_track_brace_body`, `_find_function_start`, `_detect_monster_functions`, `_detect_dead_functions`
     - Key code for `_find_function_start` (after false positive fix):
     ```python
     def _find_function_start(line: str, next_lines: list[str]) -> str | None:
         stripped = line.strip()
         if re.match(r"(?:export\s+)?(?:interface|type|enum|class)\s+", stripped):
             return None
         # function declaration
         m = re.match(r"(?:export\s+)?(?:default\s+)?(?:async\s+)?function\s+(\w+)\s*\(", stripped)
         if m:
             return m.group(1)
         # const/let/var assignment
         m = re.match(r"(?:export\s+)?(?:const|let|var)\s+(\w+)", stripped)
         if not m:
             return None
         name = m.group(1)
         combined = "\n".join([stripped] + [l.strip() for l in next_lines[:2]])
         eq_pos = combined.find("=", m.end())
         if eq_pos == -1:
             return None
         after_eq = combined[eq_pos + 1:].lstrip()
         if re.match(r"(?:async|function)\b", after_eq):
             return name
         if after_eq.startswith("("):
             brace_pos = combined.find("{", eq_pos)
             segment = combined[eq_pos:brace_pos] if brace_pos != -1 else combined[eq_pos:]
             if "=>" in segment:
                 return name
         return None
     ```

4. Errors and Fixes:
   - **TS dead_function false positives (70 → 32 hits)**: `const posterFallbackTimeoutRef = useRef<(() => void) | null>(null)` was matched as a function because `=>` in the generic type annotation. Fixed by tightening `_find_function_start` to require that after `=`, the first token must be `(`, `async`, or `function`. Function calls like `useMemo(`, `useRef(`, `React.memo(` are now correctly skipped.
   - **Path duplication bug**: On macOS, `/tmp` is a symlink to `/private/tmp`. `Path.cwd()` returns `/tmp/desloppify` but resolved file paths use `/private/tmp/desloppify`, causing `rel()` to fail and creating duplicate findings with different IDs. Fixed by resolving PROJECT_ROOT and using `.resolve()` in `rel()`.
   - **Hardcoded URL in template**: Template uses `__PLACEHOLDER__` string replacement, not f-strings. Initially tried `{D3_CDN_URL}` which wouldn't work. Changed to `__D3_CDN_URL__` placeholder pattern.
   - **Global keyword smell**: Restructured `set_exclusions` to use module self-import (`import desloppify.utils as _self; _self._extra_exclusions = patterns`) instead of `global _extra_exclusions`.

Here's my assessment of the 32 findings — which are overzealous and which are worth fixing:

---

## Overzealous Measures (not making the code better)

**1. Deferred imports (13 of 14 smell findings)** — These are ALL deliberate lazy imports. In desloppify, phase runners import detectors inside functions to: avoid circular imports between modules, lazy-load heavy dependencies, and keep the module namespace clean. This is a standard, intentional Python pattern. Fixing these would actually break the architecture. **Verdict: wontfix all 13.**

**2. Single-use command files (4 findings)** — `scan.py`, `status.py`, `detect.py`, `plan_cmd.py` are each imported only by `cli.py`. That's... how CLI command modules work. Each command lives in its own file, imported by the dispatcher. The detector is designed for shared libraries where single-use abstractions indicate over-engineering — it's not applicable to command modules. **Verdict: wontfix all 4.**

**3. Complexity-only structural findings (5 of 13)** — `cli.py` (complexity 41), `status.py` (30), `python/__init__.py` (27), `ts/fixers/vars.py` (30), `ts/detectors/react.py` (26). These files are under 300 LOC but flagged for "high complexity." CLI arg parsing is inherently verbose. Display formatting has many branches. Detection logic has pattern matching. The complexity score measures cyclomatic paths, not actual difficulty of understanding. Splitting `cli.py`'s argparse setup into multiple files would make it harder to understand, not easier. **Verdict: wontfix all 5.**

**4. Hardcoded URL (1 smell finding)** — `visualize.py` has `D3_CDN_URL = "https://d3js.org/d3.v7.min.js"` at module level. It's already extracted to a named constant. The detector just sees any `"https://..."` string. **Verdict: wontfix (already best practice).**

## Legitimate Findings (worth fixing)

**5. Large files worth splitting (5 findings):**
- `lang/typescript/detectors/smells.py` (618 LOC) — Has distinct regex, brace-tracking, and AST-like sections. Split would improve navigability.
- `commands/move.py` (486 LOC) — Complex command, let me check if it's doing multiple distinct things.
- `state.py` (389 LOC) — State loading + merging + recomputation. Could extract merging logic.
- `lang/python/extractors.py` (380 LOC) — Independent extraction functions. Each could stand alone.
- `commands/fix_cmd.py` (368 LOC) — Fix command with multiple sub-operations.

**6. Borderline large files (3 findings):**
- `visualize.py` (565 LOC) — ~400 lines is HTML template. Splitting would just move text to another file, not improve code quality. **Probably wontfix.**
- `lang/typescript/__init__.py` (435 LOC) — Phase runner definitions. One cohesive concept. **Probably wontfix.**
- `lang/base.py` (315 LOC) — Just barely over 300. **Probably wontfix.**

**7. Near-duplicate (1 finding):** `cmd_smells` in Python vs TypeScript commands (92% similar). Could extract a shared helper. **Worth fixing.**

---

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - **Desloppify**: Python CLI code quality tool, git repo at `/tmp/desloppify/`, installed editable (`pip install -e .`) so all edits to `/tmp/desloppify/desloppify/` are immediately reflected in the installed package
   - **Dimension-based scoring**: 11 dimensions, tier-weighted (T1-T4), confidence-weighted (high=1.0, medium=0.7, low=0.3)
   - **Health vs Strict scores**: Health counts only `open` as failures; Strict counts `open` + `wontfix`
   - **Potentials**: Total universe of things a detector checked; score = (potential - weighted_failures) / potential
   - **State files**: `.desloppify/state-python.json` for Python scans, requires `--state state-python.json` flag
   - **Phase pipeline**: Language configs define phases (Unused, Structural, Coupling, Smells, Dupes) that produce findings
   - **macOS symlink issue**: `/tmp` → `/private/tmp` causes path mismatches between `Path.cwd()` and `Path.resolve()`
   - **Python language default_src**: Set to `"."` (project root), TypeScript default_src: `"src"`

     ```python
     def _resolve_lang(args):
         """Resolve the language config from args, with auto-detection fallback."""
         lang_name = getattr(args, "lang", None)
         if lang_name is None:
             from .lang import auto_detect_lang
             from .utils import PROJECT_ROOT
             lang_name = auto_detect_lang(PROJECT_ROOT)
         if lang_name is None:
             return None
         from .lang import get_lang
         return get_lang(lang_name)
     ```

Here's my analysis of the real numbers from the reigh codebase:

## The Weight Distribution

| Tier | Dimensions | Weight | Share |
|------|-----------|--------|-------|
| T1 | 2 (imports, logs) | 2 | 8% |
| T2 | 1 (API surface) | 2 | 8% |
| T3 | 6 (file health, components, coupling, org, quality, patterns) | 18 | **69%** |
| T4 | 1 (cycles) | 4 | 15% |

T3 is 69% of your overall score. The score is basically "how's my T3 stuff?"

## Three Real Problems

**1. Equal-weight averaging within dimensions is wrong.**

API surface dimension: `exports` checked 1,459 things (99.9% pass) and `deprecated` checked 46 things (93.5% pass). Average = 96.7%. But if weighted by potential: 99.7%. Three deprecated usages drag the dimension from A+ to B+ because they have equal say as 1,459 exports. Same issue with Code quality — `smells` (958 files, 87.9%) and `react` (222 files, 97.5%) are averaged equally, but smells should dominate since it checked 4x more.

**2. Pattern consistency has 42 checks and equal weight to File health with 958.**

42 "areas" with 5 anomalies = 91.7%. This has the same T3 weight as File health (958 files, 92.7%). Pattern consistency is a noisy dimension based on a tiny sample. It shouldn't have equal say.

**3. Duplication potential will be quadratic.**

When the slow detector runs, duplication potential = `n*(n-1)/2` function pairs. For 500 functions that's 124,750 pairs. Even 10 real dupes = 99.99% pass rate. The dimension will always look perfect regardless of actual duplication. The potential should be `total_functions` or capped.

## What a "mega-autist" software engineer would say

> "The math is broken in predictable ways. Dimension averaging should be potential-weighted. Confidence weights (1.0/0.7/0.3) are arbitrary — where's the calibration data? Duplication potential is O(n^2), making it meaningless. Pattern consistency with 42 samples has the same influence as structural with 958. Cycles at T4 (highest) while coupling violations are T3 doesn't make sense — they're both dependency graph problems. The system rewards having many detectors with huge universes (logs: 958 files checked!) while penalizing small focused detectors (deprecated: 46 items). Fix the averaging."

## What an "artist" software engineer would say

> "The score says 94.9 and the weakest dimension is Component design at 84.3%. That tells me exactly what to focus on. I don't care if it's 94.9 vs 95.2 — the relative ordering of dimensions is what matters. The dashboard narrative is correct even if the precise numbers are slightly off. Don't make it more complicated to make it more precise. The confidence weights don't need to be perfect — they just need to be directionally right."

## The Balance

The artist is right about the narrative — the dimension breakdown is the real value and it's working well. The mega-autist is right about three specific bugs. The fix:

1. **Potential-weighted averaging within dimensions** (~5 line change in `compute_dimension_scores`)
2. **Cap duplication potential** — use `min(total_pairs, total_functions * 10)` or just `total_functions`
3. **Leave everything else** — the tier weights, confidence weights, and dimension groupings tell the right story even if they're imprecise

# A Critique of the Desloppify Scoring System

## 1. What Is Code Quality, Really?

### The health/hygiene confusion

Desloppify presents itself as measuring "health" -- the word appears throughout the UI: "Desloppify Health: 95.0/100." But what it actually measures is closer to hygiene: the absence of detectable surface-level patterns. These are not the same thing, and the conflation matters.

A codebase can have perfect hygiene -- zero unused imports, zero debug logs, zero orphaned files -- and still be deeply unhealthy. The architecture could be fundamentally wrong. The domain model could be a poor fit for the problem. The abstraction boundaries could be in the wrong places. The code could be correct but incomprehensible, or comprehensible but brittle, or neither.

Conversely, a codebase in the middle of a brilliant refactoring might temporarily have dead exports, orphaned files, and naming inconsistencies -- all of which would tank its score -- while actually being in the process of becoming better.

The tool knows this at some level. The tier system (T1 = mechanical, T4 = structural) encodes a rough gradient from hygiene to health. But the score flattens this distinction into a single number that makes no claim about which kind of quality it represents.

### What static analysis can and cannot see

There is a hierarchy of code quality concerns, roughly ordered by how much a tool can see:

1. **Syntax and formatting** -- fully automatable, already handled by linters/formatters
2. **Dead code and unused symbols** -- detectable with high confidence
3. **Structural metrics** (file size, complexity scores, coupling counts) -- detectable, but whether they indicate problems requires judgment
4. **Design patterns and anti-patterns** (god components, passthrough abstractions, state sync issues) -- partially detectable with heuristics, high false-positive risk
5. **Abstraction quality** (are the right concepts named? do the boundaries reflect the domain?) -- not detectable
6. **Narrative clarity** (can a new developer read this code and understand the system?) -- not detectable
7. **Fitness for purpose** (does the architecture support the kind of changes this codebase will actually need?) -- not detectable
8. **Taste** (does this code feel right?) -- by definition not detectable

Desloppify operates primarily in layers 2-4, with tentative forays into layer 5 via coupling detection and pattern consistency. This is an honest scope, and more ambitious than most linters. But the score is presented with the confidence of a comprehensive health assessment, when it really covers only the lower strata of the quality hierarchy.

### The 95.0 illusion

The real codebase scores 95.0/100. A developer seeing this number learns almost nothing. Is the codebase excellent? Or is it merely clean? The number cannot distinguish between "this code is well-architected and the detectors found little to complain about" and "this codebase has 451 open findings but they are diluted across 958 files worth of potentials so the pass rates are high."

The second interpretation is what is actually happening. There are 451 open findings. The codebase has 182,541 lines of code across 958 files. The score is high because the potentials (the denominators) are large. Component design scores 84.3% with 97 issues across only 447 checks. That is genuinely concerning. But the overall score buries this signal by averaging it with dimensions where the denominator is enormous (Coupling: 3,200 checks, 36 issues; Dependency health: 3,942 checks, 4 issues).

## 2. The Human Experience

### What does 95.0/100 feel?

A developer sees "95.0/100" and thinks one of two things:

- "Great, we're nearly done" -- which is wrong, because there are 451 open issues
- "This number is meaningless" -- which is also wrong, because the dimension breakdown contains real signal

Neither reaction is useful. The number creates a false sense of either completion or irrelevance. The dimension breakdown is where the actual value lives, but the headline number actively undermines it by suggesting the breakdown is merely "details" below a summary that already tells the story.

### The dimension breakdown: better, but still compressed

The dimension table is genuinely more useful than the overall score. "Component design: 84.3%" with 97 issues tells a developer something actionable: there is a systemic problem with component interfaces in this codebase. "Pattern consistency: 91.7%" with only 5 issues over 42 checks tells a different story: this dimension has low confidence because the sample is small.

But the table does not tell these stories itself. A developer has to know that 42 checks means "we barely looked at this" while 3,200 checks means "this is well-sampled." The table shows checks as a number, but the visual weight of the score column dominates. Pattern consistency at 91.7% looks better than Component design at 84.3%, when in reality the former is a weak signal over a tiny sample and the latter is a strong signal over a substantial one.

### Goodhart's Law: when the score becomes the goal

The most dangerous property of this scoring system is that it is legible to AI agents. The tool already targets an agent-assisted workflow -- the scan output includes "Create a task doc for each area; farm to sub-agents for decomposition." This means AI agents will be optimizing this score.

An AI agent optimizing desloppify's score will:
- Remove console.log statements (good, trivially)
- Remove unused imports (good, trivially)
- Inline single-use abstractions or add spurious second importers (bad -- gaming single_use detection)
- Split large files mechanically at arbitrary boundaries to reduce file size (bad -- the new files may be less coherent than the large one)
- Reduce prop interface sizes by passing objects instead of individual props (unclear -- could be better or worse depending on the domain)
- Extract "duplicate" functions into shared utilities that add coupling without adding clarity (bad)

The problem is structural: every detector has a clear "pass" condition, and an agent will find the cheapest path to that condition, which is often not the path that improves code quality. The tool's `compute_score_impact` function -- which estimates the point improvement from fixing N issues -- is essentially a guide for gaming the score. It tells you where to allocate effort for maximum numerical return, not maximum quality return.

### When following the tool makes code worse

Several specific scenarios:

**Single-use findings.** The tool flags files that are imported by only one other file, with a size filter (too small or too large = flagged, 50-200 LOC = fine). But single-use abstractions are often exactly right. A well-named module that encapsulates one coherent concept and is used in one place is not a problem -- it is good design. The suppression heuristic (co-located files, appropriate size) catches many false positives, but the philosophical framing is wrong. The tool treats single-use as inherently suspect and defines a "normal" range. Good code often has many single-use modules by design.

**Structural/complexity findings.** A file with 15+ imports, 3+ useEffects, and 500+ lines gets flagged for decomposition. But sometimes a component legitimately orchestrates many concerns. Splitting it into five files that each manage one concern but need to coordinate through shared state or callbacks can make the code harder to understand, not easier. The tool counts signals but cannot assess whether the current decomposition is already the least-bad option.

**Duplicate detection.** Two functions with high structural similarity may be intentionally separate because they serve different domains and will diverge over time. Merging them into a shared utility creates coupling between domains, which is a different (and arguably worse) kind of code quality problem. The tool detects duplication in one dimension but is blind to the coupling it would create in another.

## 3. What Is Missing from the Model

### Dimensions of quality this tool cannot see

**Naming quality.** The tool detects naming *inconsistency* (camelCase vs. PascalCase in the same directory), but not naming *quality*. Whether `useStructureVideo` is a good name -- whether it communicates what the hook does, whether it would make sense to a new developer -- is the more important question and is invisible to static analysis.

**Conceptual coherence.** Does each file do one thing? Not "is it under 500 lines" (a proxy), but does it represent a single coherent concept that a developer can hold in their head? A 200-line file that mixes three concerns is worse than a 600-line file that thoroughly handles one concern. The tool uses file size as a proxy for coherence, which sometimes correlates and sometimes does not.

**API design quality.** The tool detects props bloat (too many props) and passthrough components (most props forwarded). These are real signals. But it cannot assess whether the API is well-designed -- whether the prop names make sense, whether the component's contract is predictable, whether the interface is stable or churns with every feature addition.

**Test coverage and test quality.** The tool does not measure whether the code is tested, or whether the tests actually verify meaningful behavior. This is arguably more important for long-term health than any dimension the tool currently tracks.

**Documentation at decision points.** Comments explaining *why* (not what) are among the highest-value additions to a codebase. The tool detects TODOs as a complexity signal but cannot assess whether critical decisions are documented.

**Change velocity and churn.** Files that change frequently may need more attention than files that are stable. A "messy" file that never changes may be fine; a "clean" file that changes every sprint may need better abstractions. Git history is a rich signal that the tool ignores entirely.

### Is code quality even one thing?

No. The tool implicitly assumes code quality is a scalar -- you can reduce it to a number between 0 and 100. But code quality is at minimum a vector with several incommensurable dimensions:

- **Correctness**: Does it do what it should? (Not measurable by this tool.)
- **Comprehensibility**: Can a human read it? (Partially proxied by complexity scores.)
- **Maintainability**: Can it be changed safely? (Partially proxied by coupling metrics.)
- **Performance**: Does it run efficiently? (Not measured.)
- **Resilience**: Does it handle failures gracefully? (Not measured.)
- **Deletability**: Can parts be removed cleanly? (Partially proxied by coupling/orphaned metrics.)

These dimensions trade off against each other. Highly resilient code (with extensive error handling) is often less readable. Highly performant code often has higher coupling. A single score cannot capture these trade-offs; it can only collapse them.

### What would a full-picture scoring system look like?

It would not produce a single number. It would produce a profile -- more like a medical report than a GPA. "Your coupling is low, your files are well-sized, but we notice high component complexity and no test coverage in these areas. Here are the three things that would most improve your ability to ship features safely."

It would also incorporate temporal data: "This file has been modified 47 times in the last 6 months but has no tests. This other file has 5 complexity signals but has not changed in a year."

And it would incorporate human judgment explicitly: "You told us that the video tool is the area where you add features most often. Given that, here is where to focus."

## 4. The Dimension Choices

### Do the 11 dimensions carve reality at the joints?

Partially. Some dimensions are natural and coherent:

- **Import hygiene** (T1): Clear, narrow, automatable. Good dimension.
- **Debug cleanliness** (T1): Same. Clean, scoped, actionable.
- **Dependency health** (T4, cycles): Well-defined, important, hard to fix. Good dimension.

Some dimensions are grab-bags that conflate distinct concerns:

- **Code quality** (T3, detectors: smells + react): This dimension is named after the thing the entire tool purports to measure. It lumps "code smells" (a heterogeneous category that includes everything from long if-chains to magic numbers) with "React state sync anti-patterns" (a very specific framework concern). The name is too broad to be informative, and the contents are too diverse to be coherent.

- **Organization** (T3, detectors: orphaned + flat_dirs + naming): Orphaned files (nobody imports them), flat directories (too many files in one dir), and naming inconsistencies are three very different problems. An orphaned file might need to be deleted or connected. A flat directory needs subdirectories. Naming inconsistency needs a renaming pass. Lumping them together means the dimension score does not tell you which kind of organizational problem you have.

- **Coupling** (T3, detectors: single_use + coupling): Single-use abstractions and coupling violations are related but distinct. A single-use file in `shared/` that is only imported by one tool is a coupling/boundary problem. A single-use file that is co-located with its consumer is a design choice. The dimension treats them as the same kind of problem.

### Natural clusters that the model misses

**"Readability" is not a dimension.** Complexity signals, naming, props bloat, and code smells all contribute to readability, but they are scattered across File health, Organization, Component design, and Code quality. There is no single dimension that answers "how readable is this codebase?"

**"Architecture" is not a dimension.** Cycles, coupling violations, boundary candidates, and single-use abstractions all relate to architectural structure, but they are split across Coupling and Dependency health. The former is T3 and the latter is T4, which means a mild coupling violation and a 3-file import cycle are weighted differently even though they may represent similar architectural problems.

### The tier system's implicit philosophy

The tier weights (T1=1, T2=2, T3=3, T4=4) encode a specific belief: structural problems are 4x more important than mechanical ones. This is defensible but not self-evident. 

Consider: 50 unused imports (T1) might indicate a systemic problem with how the team manages dependencies -- a process failure that no amount of one-time cleanup will fix. Meanwhile, one import cycle (T4) might be a known, contained compromise that the team has decided to live with. The tier system would weight the cycle 4x more than each import, even though the imports may represent the more actionable systemic issue.

The tier system also creates a perverse incentive structure in the overall score. Because T3 has the most dimensions (6 of 11) and they all carry weight 3, the overall score is dominated by T3 concerns. T1 dimensions (Import hygiene, Debug cleanliness) together contribute weight 2 to the total, while T3 dimensions contribute weight 18. This means T1 issues have almost no effect on the headline number, which means the tool gives no numerical credit for fixing them -- even though they are the easiest to fix and provide immediate, guaranteed improvement.

The real codebase demonstrates this: Import hygiene is 99.5% (5 issues out of 958 checks) and Debug cleanliness is 99.4% (6 issues), but fixing all 11 of these T1 issues would barely move the overall score because their weight in the total is negligible. This is demotivating. The tool's "quick wins first" philosophy in its suggested workflow contradicts its scoring system's "quick wins barely count" mathematics.

### The philosophy this tier system endorses

The tier system says: "What matters most about code is its structural integrity. Mechanical cleanliness is a nice-to-have. The hard, judgment-requiring work is the real work."

This is a reasonable philosophy for an experienced engineer working on a mature codebase. It is a poor philosophy for a junior developer trying to build good habits, or for a team that has let mechanical debt accumulate to the point where it obscures structural problems. It is also a philosophy that discourages the kind of incremental, low-risk improvement that builds momentum.

## 5. What Would I Change

### Stop presenting a single number

The most impactful change would be to stop computing an overall score entirely, or at least stop making it the headline. Replace "Health: 95.0/100" with a narrative summary:

> **3 areas need attention:** Component design has 97 issues (mostly props bloat and passthrough components). File health has 91 issues (large/complex files, mainly in the video tool). Code smells are scattered across 187 findings. Everything else is clean.

This tells the developer what the tool actually knows, without collapsing it into a number that invites misinterpretation.

### Separate confidence from importance

The current system conflates two things in its dimension scores: how severe the problems are and how much data the tool has. Pattern consistency at 91.7% with 42 checks looks like a middling result. But the real story is: "We could barely measure this. The 5 findings we have might be significant or might be noise." The display should communicate statistical confidence separately from the score. A dimension with fewer than ~100 checks should be flagged as "low confidence" rather than presented with the same visual weight as one with 3,000 checks.

### Reweight the tiers, or abandon scalar weighting

If you keep a single score, T1 and T2 should contribute more. Not because they are more important per-issue, but because they are more actionable. The current system says "your score is 95 and the things you can easily fix won't change it." A better system would make the score responsive to any improvement, so that fixing 11 easy T1 issues feels rewarding even if the deep structural work is where the real value lives.

Alternatively, abandon scalar weighting and show separate "tracks": a cleanliness track (T1+T2, is the codebase tidy?), a structure track (T3, are the abstractions right?), and an architecture track (T4, are the dependencies healthy?). Let the developer decide which track matters most for their current situation.

### Add temporal context

The tool already tracks `first_seen`, `last_seen`, and `reopen_count`. Use this data. A finding that has been open for 6 months is different from one that appeared yesterday. A finding that keeps reopening (chronic reopeners) is different from a stable finding. The score should reflect this: stale findings should either escalate (they matter and nobody is fixing them) or fade (they have been reviewed and deemed acceptable).

### Make the agent-optimization problem explicit

If the tool is designed for agent-assisted workflows, it needs to be adversarially robust. Currently, an agent can inflate the score by:
- Removing console.logs (trivial, always improves score)
- Adding a second import of a single-use file from a test file (removes the finding, adds a meaningless import)
- Splitting a 600-line file into two 300-line files at an arbitrary point (reduces structural findings without improving structure)

The tool should either detect these gaming patterns or reframe its output to discourage them. One approach: instead of "fix 97 items for +X points," provide specific, opinionated guidance: "This component has 12 props, 8 of which are forwarded. Consider whether this component should exist, or whether the parent should render the child directly." The guidance should make the agent do the thinking, not the counting.

### Rethink what "potential" means

The potentials concept is the mathematical backbone of the scoring system: each detector has a denominator (total things checked) and the score is (checked - failed) / checked. But the potentials are not equally meaningful across detectors.

For `unused` imports, the potential is total files scanned. An unused import in file A and a clean file B both contribute to the denominator. This is reasonable: more files = more opportunity for unused imports.

For `props`, the potential is the number of component interfaces found. But a codebase with many small, focused components will have a high potential and likely a high pass rate (small interfaces are rarely bloated). A codebase with fewer, larger components will have a lower potential and a lower pass rate. The denominator rewards the very design choice (many small components) that the dimension is supposed to encourage. This creates a ceiling effect where well-designed codebases score high by definition and the score provides no further signal.

For `patterns`, the potential is 42 (total areas checked). This is a tiny number compared to other detectors. Five findings over 42 checks produces 91.7%, while five findings over 958 checks would produce 99.5%. The dimension score is dominated by the denominator, which is an artifact of how many "areas" the codebase happens to have, not a measure of pattern quality.

The potentials should be normalized or the scoring should account for the vastly different scales. A dimension with 42 checks should not have the same visual and mathematical weight as one with 3,942 checks.

### Embrace subjectivity, or be explicit about objectivity's limits

The tool tries hard to be objective: potentials, pass rates, confidence weights, tier weights. This is admirable and necessary for automation. But it creates a false sense of comprehensiveness. The things the tool measures are the things that are easy to measure, not necessarily the things that matter most.

The most valuable version of this tool would be honest about its limits. Not "Health: 95.0/100" but "Hygiene: 95.0/100 -- this measures detectable surface issues. For deeper code quality assessment, consider: [list of things a human should review]." The tool should position itself as a first pass that handles the mechanical work so that human judgment can focus on the hard problems. Currently it presents itself as a comprehensive health assessment, which it is not and cannot be.

### The ideal relationship between tool and developer

The tool should be an opinionated colleague, not an authoritative judge. It should say "I notice this pattern, and in my experience it correlates with problems" rather than "your score is 95.0 and here is what to fix to make it 96.0." It should surface information and let the developer decide what matters, rather than pre-deciding through weights and averages.

The dimension breakdown already gestures toward this: showing the developer where different kinds of issues cluster. The overall score undermines it by collapsing that nuance into a number. The number is seductive because it is simple, but that simplicity is exactly the problem. Code quality is not simple, and a tool that pretends it is will eventually train developers (and their AI assistants) to mistake the map for the territory.

---

## Summary

Desloppify is a well-built tool that does real work: it finds genuine problems (unused code, overly complex files, architectural coupling) and tracks them over time. The detector suite is thoughtful, the suppression heuristics show good taste, and the workflow integration (scan, review, fix, rescan) is sound.

The scoring system is where the abstraction breaks down. The single headline number (95.0/100) communicates less than the dimension breakdown that produces it. The tier weighting, while philosophically defensible, creates perverse incentives by making easy wins nearly invisible in the score. The potentials-based pass rates conflate "we looked at a lot of things and found few problems" with "the problems we found are minor." And the entire system is vulnerable to Goodhart's Law -- agents optimizing the score will make the number go up without necessarily making the code better.

The deepest issue is ontological: the tool treats code quality as a scalar, but it is a multidimensional, context-dependent, partially-subjective property that resists quantification. The best version of this tool would embrace that resistance -- providing rich, narrative, dimension-specific guidance rather than collapsing everything into a number that feels precise but is not.</result>

## The Mega-Autist Critique (precision-focused)

**The deepest problem: denominators lie.** The score = `(potential - failures) / potential` means the score is dominated by the denominator, not the severity of failures.

- **Cycles**: 4 cycles / 3,942 dep edges = 99.9%. But "edges" is the wrong unit. Nobody asks "what fraction of my import edges are in cycles?" The right denominator is modules (maybe 12-16 files participate in cycles out of 958 = 98.3%). The inflated denominator makes cycles look trivially healthy.
- **Coupling**: 7 violations / 2,711 edges = 99.8%. But most edges (importing `lodash`, importing `React`) are irrelevant. Only boundary-crossing edges matter. The denominator should be edges that *could* violate a boundary.
- **Smells**: 187 findings / 958 files = 87.9%. But findings are per (file, smell_id) while potential is per-file. One file with 5 smells produces 5 weighted failures against potential contribution of 1. The formula can go negative (clamped to 0).

**Score goes UP when code gets WORSE:** Adding empty/clean files inflates denominators. Deleting a problematic module removes both findings and potential. The score rewards bloat and punishes trimming.

**Score goes DOWN when code gets BETTER:** Deleting dead code removes clean files from denominators. Adding `@deprecated` annotations creates findings. Running slow detectors for the first time adds a new dimension with issues.

**The confidence weights (1.0/0.7/0.3) conflate two things:** detection accuracy ("how sure is this real?") and severity ("how bad if real?"). A low-confidence god component and a high-confidence naming inconsistency shouldn't use the same scale.

**Concrete recommendations:** Fix cycles/coupling denominators. Add completeness indicator when phases are skipped. Dampen small-sample dimensions (patterns: 42 checks). Separate severity from detection confidence. Consider `score = 100 * exp(-k * failures / sqrt(potential))` instead of linear pass-rate.

---

## The Artist Critique (holistic/taste-focused)

**The deepest problem: the score measures hygiene, not health.** A codebase can score 100% by having zero detectable patterns while being fundamentally mis-architected. The tool measures *absence of detected slop* (honest — the name says "desloppify") but presents it as *health* (dishonest — "Health: 95.0/100").

**The hierarchy of what matters:**
1. Syntax/formatting (fully automatable)
2. Dead code (detectable)
3. Structural metrics (detectable, but whether they indicate problems requires judgment)
4. Design patterns (partially detectable, high false-positive risk)
5. Abstraction quality (not detectable)
6. Narrative clarity (not detectable)
7. Fitness for purpose (not detectable)
8. Taste (by definition not detectable)

The tool operates in layers 2-4 but presents with the confidence of a comprehensive assessment.

**95.0/100 tells the wrong story.** There are 451 open findings. 292 of them (65%) are in just two dimensions (Component design + Code quality). Someone reading "95.0" would never know this. The dimension breakdown contains real signal, but the headline number undermines it by suggesting the breakdown is "details."

**Goodhart's Law is the real threat.** AI agents will optimize this score by: removing console.logs (good), splitting large files at arbitrary points (bad), adding spurious second imports to eliminate single-use findings (bad), extracting "duplicate" functions into shared utilities that add coupling (bad). The tool's `compute_score_impact` function is literally a guide for gaming the score.

**When following the tool makes code worse:** Single-use abstractions are often exactly right (a well-named module used in one place is good design). Splitting a file that orchestrates many concerns can make it harder to understand. Deduplicating two similar functions creates coupling between domains.

**What's missing entirely:** Naming quality (not inconsistency — actual clarity), conceptual coherence, API design quality, test coverage, documentation at decision points, change velocity/churn from git history.

**The tier system contradicts the workflow.** T1 (unused imports, debug logs) has weight 1. Fixing all 11 T1 issues would barely move the score. But the tool's suggested workflow is "quick wins first." The math says quick wins don't count. This is demotivating.

**Concrete recommendations:** Stop presenting a single number (or at least stop making it the headline). Show statistical confidence separately. Reweight tiers so easy wins feel rewarding. Add temporal context (stale findings should escalate). Make guidance opinionated ("consider whether this component should exist") not numerical ("fix 97 items for +X points"). Position the tool as a first pass, not a comprehensive assessment.

---

## Where They Agree

Both perspectives identify the same core problems:
1. **Inflated denominators** (cycles, coupling) inflate the score
2. **The single number is misleading** — the dimension breakdown is where the value lives
3. **Small-sample dimensions** (patterns: 42 checks) shouldn't have equal weight
4. **The smells unit mismatch** (findings per file vs. files as denominator)

## The Sensible Middle

The most impactful fixes that both sides would endorse:

1. **Fix cycle and coupling denominators** — use files-in-cycles and boundary-crossing-edges, not all edges
2. **Dampen small-sample dimensions** — weight by `min(1, potential/200)` 
3. **Add completeness indicator** — flag when slow phases were skipped
4. **Fix the smells unit mismatch** — cap per-file weighted failures at 1.0
5. **Show the dimension breakdown more prominently** — it's better than the number
