# Critique of the Desloppify Scoring System

## Level 1: Philosophical Foundations

### What does "code health" even mean?

The system implicitly defines code health as **the absence of detectable anti-patterns relative to the size of the codebase**. This is a subtraction model: you start at 100 and lose points for problems found. The philosophical assumption is that code is healthy by default and degrades as specific pathologies accumulate.

This is a defensible framing for a linter-like tool, but it encodes a specific and debatable worldview: that quality is about avoiding known-bad patterns rather than achieving known-good properties. A codebase could score 100/100 by having zero detectable anti-patterns while being fundamentally poorly architected in ways none of the detectors can see. The score measures **absence of detected slop**, not **presence of quality**. These are different things.

The name "desloppify" is actually honest about this. It is measuring slop. The danger is when the number 95.0 is interpreted as "this codebase is 95% healthy" rather than "we found issues in about 5% of the checkable surface area."

### Is a single number meaningful or misleading?

A single number (95.0/100) is inherently misleading because it collapses a multi-dimensional reality into a scalar. Consider the real data from the Reigh codebase:

| Dimension | Score | Checks | Issues |
|---|---|---|---|
| Import hygiene | 99.5 | 958 | 5 |
| Debug cleanliness | 99.4 | 958 | 6 |
| API surface | 99.7 | 1505 | 4 |
| File health | 92.7 | 958 | 91 |
| Component design | 84.3 | 447 | 97 |
| Coupling | 99.2 | 3200 | 36 |
| Organization | 99.6 | 1259 | 8 |
| Code quality | 89.7 | 1180 | 195 |
| Pattern consistency | 91.7 | 42 | 5 |
| Dependency health | 99.9 | 3942 | 4 |

The overall 95.0 conceals that Component design is at 84.3 with 97 issues and Code quality has 195 issues. Someone glancing at "95.0" would not know that nearly half of all findings (292/451) are concentrated in two dimensions. The single number optimizes for feeling good, not for actionable understanding.

That said, a single number has motivational utility. The dimension breakdown is there for anyone who looks. The question is whether the aggregation formula produces a number that moves in the right direction when the code changes. As we will see, it often does not.

### What is the implicit model of quality?

The model is: **quality = weighted average of per-dimension pass rates, where each dimension's pass rate is (checkable things minus weighted failures) / checkable things.**

This is essentially a weighted-defect-density model. It treats all checkable items as equally important within a dimension and uses tier weights to express that structural problems matter more than lint. The confidence weights (1.0/0.7/0.3) attempt to handle uncertainty in detection.

The model assumes that:
1. Quality decomposes cleanly into independent dimensions.
2. Within a dimension, issues are roughly fungible.
3. The ratio of (issues found / things checked) is a meaningful signal.
4. Tier weights correctly express relative importance.

Assumption (1) is mostly fine for practical purposes. Assumption (2) is very questionable -- a god component with 15 hooks is not the same severity as a file that is 501 LOC. Assumption (3) is where the deepest problems lie, as detailed below. Assumption (4) is at least explicit and tunable.

---

## Level 2: Mathematical Model

### Is `(potential - weighted_failures) / potential` the right formula?

This is the pass-rate formula, and it has a fundamental structural property: **the score is dominated by the denominator (potential), not the numerator (failures).** If potential is large and failures are small, you get a high score regardless of the severity of those failures. If potential is small, even minor failures cause large swings.

Concretely, from the real data:

- **Cycles**: 4 issues / 3942 checks = 99.9%. The denominator is "total edges in the dependency graph." Having 3942 edges and 4 cycles makes cycles look trivial. But an import cycle can be a fundamental architectural problem that makes the entire codebase harder to reason about. Four cycles in a 958-file codebase is not "99.9% healthy" in any meaningful sense.

- **Component design**: 97 issues / 447 checks = 84.3%. The denominator is `max(prop_count, len(pt_entries))`. This is a much tighter denominator, so each issue carries more weight. Is component design really 15 percentage points worse than file health? Or is the denominator just calibrated differently?

The formula is biased toward high scores whenever the denominator is large relative to the numerator. This is not an accident -- it's a natural property of ratio metrics. But it means the score is more a measure of "how many things didn't we flag" than "how serious are the things we did flag."

### Are the confidence weights defensible?

The weights are: high=1.0, medium=0.7, low=0.3.

These are ordinal-to-interval mappings with no empirical basis. The gap between high and medium (0.3) equals the entire weight of low. Is a low-confidence finding really 30% of a high-confidence one? The answer depends entirely on the detector's false-positive rate at each confidence level, which is unknown and varies by detector.

More problematically, confidence is used as both a measure of **detection accuracy** (how likely is this a real problem?) and **severity** (how bad is this if real?). These are conflated. A low-confidence structural finding (maybe this file is too complex) could be a massive problem if real, while a high-confidence naming inconsistency is definitionally trivial.

The practical effect: the confidence weights slightly deflate scores for detectors that produce medium/low confidence findings. This is modest -- the difference between counting 97 issues at weight 1.0 vs weight 0.7 is the difference between 78.3% and 84.7% in the Component design dimension. It matters, but it's not catastrophic.

### Are the tier weights defensible?

Tier weights: {1: 1, 2: 2, 3: 3, 4: 4}. Linear scale from 1-4.

This means tier-4 dimensions (Dependency health) get 4x the weight of tier-1 dimensions (Import hygiene, Debug cleanliness). Let us compute the actual weight distribution from the real data:

| Dimension | Tier | Weight | Weighted contribution |
|---|---|---|---|
| Import hygiene | 1 | 1 | 99.5 * 1 = 99.5 |
| Debug cleanliness | 1 | 1 | 99.4 * 1 = 99.4 |
| API surface | 2 | 2 | 99.7 * 2 = 199.4 |
| File health | 3 | 3 | 92.7 * 3 = 278.1 |
| Component design | 3 | 3 | 84.3 * 3 = 252.9 |
| Coupling | 3 | 3 | 99.2 * 3 = 297.6 |
| Organization | 3 | 3 | 99.6 * 3 = 298.8 |
| Code quality | 3 | 3 | 89.7 * 3 = 269.1 |
| Pattern consistency | 3 | 3 | 91.7 * 3 = 275.1 |
| Dependency health | 4 | 4 | 99.9 * 4 = 399.6 |

Total weight: 1+1+2+3+3+3+3+3+3+4 = 26. Weighted sum: 2469.5. Score: 2469.5/26 = 95.0.

The problem: **tier-3 dimensions are 6 out of 10 dimensions and consume 18/26 = 69% of the total weight.** Tier-3 is the "everything else" bucket. The tier system was designed to differentiate importance, but by putting most dimensions in tier-3, it effectively makes the score a slightly-weighted average where Import hygiene and Debug cleanliness are down-weighted and Dependency health is up-weighted, but the middle is flat.

The linear 1-2-3-4 scale is also odd. If tier 4 is "architectural health that affects everything," should it really be only 4x a lint check? The answer depends on what you want the score to communicate. If a single import cycle can make an entire module untestable, maybe tier 4 should be 10x tier 1. But then a single cycle would tank the score, which might be too punitive for a motivational metric.

### Does potential-weighted averaging within dimensions make sense?

When a dimension has multiple detectors (e.g., Coupling has single_use with potential=489 and coupling with potential=2711), the dimension score is computed by pooling all checks and failures into one big ratio. This means the detector with the larger potential dominates.

In the Coupling dimension: single_use has 29 issues with 20.3 weighted failures against 489 potential (95.8% pass rate), while coupling has 7 issues with 6.7 weighted failures against 2711 potential (99.8% pass rate). The pooled score is (3200 - 27.0) / 3200 = 99.2%.

The coupling detector's enormous denominator (2711 edges) drowns out the single_use signal. If you care about single-use abstractions, this pooling is harmful -- the 29 single-use findings contribute almost nothing to the dimension score because they're diluted by 2711 coupling edges where nothing was found.

An alternative would be equal-weighted detector averaging within a dimension: (95.8% + 99.8%) / 2 = 97.8%. This would give single_use findings more influence. The current approach is basically saying "the Coupling dimension checked 3200 things and found problems in 27 of them" -- which is technically true but hides the heterogeneity.

### How does the score behave as the codebase grows?

This is perhaps the most important mathematical property and it reveals a critical flaw.

**As the codebase grows, potential grows.** If you add 100 clean files, `structural` potential goes from 958 to 1058, `unused` goes from 958 to 1058, etc. The existing 91 structural issues are now 91/1058 = 91.4% instead of 91/958 = 90.5%. **The score goes up without fixing anything.** You got healthier by adding code.

Conversely, **if you delete clean files (refactoring, removing dead code), the score goes down.** You removed code that was contributing to the denominator without contributing to the numerator. The codebase is genuinely better -- smaller, less to maintain -- but the score penalizes you for it.

This is a fundamental pathology of ratio-based metrics. The score rewards bloat and punishes trimming.

**As the codebase changes character:** If you rewrite a section from many small files to fewer large files, structural potential drops but structural findings might increase (larger files trigger complexity detectors). The score could swing in either direction for reasons unrelated to actual quality.

---

## Level 3: Detector-Level Analysis

### Per-dimension potential analysis

**Import hygiene (potential=958, issues=5)**
- Potential = total files scanned. Denominator: one check per file for unused imports.
- This is reasonable. Each file either has unused imports or it doesn't. A 99.5% pass rate on 958 files means 5 files have issues. This is well-calibrated.
- Risk: trivially achievable at 100% with an auto-fixer (and the tool has one). Once you auto-fix, this dimension contributes weight to the overall score as a permanent 100%, slightly inflating it.

**Debug cleanliness (potential=958, issues=6)**
- Same denominator (total files). But the detector finds tagged debug logs, grouped by (file, tag). 
- Reasonably calibrated. Same auto-fix risk as above.

**API surface (potential=1505, issues=4)**
- Two detectors: exports (potential=1459, total exports in codebase) and deprecated (potential=46, total deprecated symbols).
- Exports: 1459 exports, 1 dead export found. 99.9% pass rate. The denominator is defensible (each export is a checkable thing), but the result is that dead exports barely move the score. Even 20 dead exports would only bring this to 98.6%.
- Deprecated: 46 deprecated symbols, 3 still have importers. This is tighter -- 3/46 = 93.5% -- but it gets drowned by the 1459-denominator exports detector in the pooled average (the dimension score is 99.7%, not 93.5%).
- **This is a concrete case where potential-weighted pooling hides real problems.** The deprecated detector is flagging something meaningful (deprecated APIs still in use), but its signal is diluted 30:1 by the exports detector.

**File health (potential=958, issues=91)**
- Potential = total files. Each file either has structural problems or it doesn't.
- Reasonable denominator. The 69.7 weighted failures (vs 91 issues) show the confidence weighting at work -- many structural findings are medium confidence, so they count as 0.7 each.
- Score of 92.7% means about 7% of files are structurally problematic. This feels about right for a mid-sized codebase.

**Component design (potential=447, issues=97)**
- Potential = max(prop_count, len(pt_entries)). This is the number of prop interfaces or passthrough components detected.
- **This denominator is unstable.** It depends on how many prop interfaces the extractor finds, which depends on coding style (inline vs extracted types). If you move prop types from inline to separate type files, the denominator could change without the underlying quality changing.
- 97 issues against 447 checks = 84.3%. This is the lowest dimension score. With 70.0 weighted failures, the confidence weighting knocks about 30% off the issue count.
- The low score here is partly real (lots of prop bloat) but partly an artifact of the tighter denominator. If props potential were 958 (total files) instead of 447, the score would be 92.7%.

**Coupling (potential=3200, issues=36)**
- Two detectors: single_use (489) + coupling (2711).
- Coupling potential = coupling_edges + cross_edges = 2711. This is the total number of import edges checked for boundary violations. Enormous denominator. 7 violations against 2711 edges = 99.8%.
- **The coupling denominator is conceptually wrong.** The question is not "what fraction of import edges violate boundaries?" but "are there boundary violations?" An edge that imports `lodash` is not a "check" for coupling in any meaningful sense. Only edges that cross specific boundaries are relevant. The denominator should be the number of edges that *could* violate a boundary (e.g., edges from shared/ to anywhere), not all edges.
- Single-use potential (489) = candidate files for single-use analysis. More reasonable as a denominator.

**Organization (potential=1259, issues=8)**
- Three detectors: orphaned (949), flat_dirs (165), naming (145).
- Orphaned: 949 files in the dep graph, 0 orphans. Clean 100%.
- Flat_dirs: 165 directories, 8 too flat. 96.6%. Reasonable denominator.
- Naming: 145 directories, 0 naming issues. Clean 100%.
- The pooled 99.6% is dominated by the 949-file orphaned check, which found nothing. The 8 flat_dirs findings contribute almost nothing to the dimension score.

**Code quality (potential=1180, issues=195)**
- Two detectors: smells (958) + react (222).
- Smells: 958 files, 187 smell findings, 115.7 weighted failures. This is the highest issue count in the entire scan.
- React: 222 effects, 8 state-sync anti-patterns. 
- Score: 89.7%. The smells detector dominates. 187 smell findings in 958 files -- but a single file can have multiple smell findings. **The potential (files) and the finding count (per-file-per-smell) are in different units.** If one file has 5 smells, that's 5 weighted failures against a potential of 1 (since the file is counted once in the denominator). This can push weighted_failures above potential, which the `max(0.0, ...)` clamp catches, but it means the formula breaks down for dimensions where a single entity can generate multiple findings.
- The current data: 115.7 weighted failures against 958 potential = 87.9%. This means smell findings, collectively, account for ~12% of files. But 187 findings across 958 files might actually affect only 50 files, each with multiple smells. The denominator doesn't know.

**Pattern consistency (potential=42, issues=5)**
- 42 total areas checked, 5 anomalies. 91.7%.
- **This is the most fragile dimension.** With only 42 checks, each finding moves the score by ~2.4 percentage points. A single new finding drops it to ~89.3%; fixing one raises it to ~94.0%. The score is highly volatile relative to other dimensions.
- More importantly: 42 checks have fundamentally different statistical properties than 3942 checks. The confidence interval around 91.7% with n=42 is enormous (roughly +/-8% at 95% confidence). The score pretends it has the same precision as the Dependency health score at 99.9% with n=3942. It does not.

**Dependency health (potential=3942, issues=4)**
- 3942 edges in the dependency graph, 4 import cycles.
- **This denominator is deeply misleading.** An import cycle involves multiple edges, but each cycle is one finding. 4 cycles each involving 3 files means maybe 12 edges participate in cycles. But the denominator counts every edge, including `import React from 'react'`. The 99.9% score says "99.9% of import edges are not in cycles," but nobody thinks about cycles that way.
- The question that matters is: "How many of my modules are entangled in dependency cycles?" Against 958 files, 4 cycles probably involve ~12-16 files, so the answer is ~1.5%. This is still low, but it's 1.5%, not 0.1%.
- Because this dimension is tier-4 (weight=4), it gets the highest weight in the final score. And because it has the largest denominator, it's nearly guaranteed to be near 100%. **Tier-4 weight times inflated-denominator produces a permanently high-scoring dimension that pulls the overall score up.** This is the single largest source of score inflation in the system.

**Duplication (not present)**
- Marked `slow=True`, so it wasn't run. This is a missing dimension entirely. The score of 95.0 is computed without any duplication data. If the duplication scan found 50 near-duplicates, the score would drop. The score is therefore conditional on which phases were run, which is not obvious to the reader.

### Summary of denominator quality

| Detector | Potential | Semantics | Calibration |
|---|---|---|---|
| unused | 958 | files | Good |
| logs | 958 | files | Good |
| exports | 1459 | total exports | Inflated (most exports are obviously live) |
| deprecated | 46 | deprecated symbols | Good |
| structural | 958 | files | Good |
| flat_dirs | 165 | directories | Good |
| props | 447 | prop interfaces | Unstable (depends on style) |
| single_use | 489 | candidate files | Reasonable |
| coupling | 2711 | import edges | Badly inflated |
| cycles | 3942 | dep graph edges | Badly inflated |
| orphaned | 949 | graph files | Good |
| patterns | 42 | areas | Too small, volatile |
| naming | 145 | directories | Good |
| smells | 958 | files | Wrong unit (findings per file, not per check) |
| react | 222 | effects | Good |
| dupes | (missing) | total functions | (not run) |

---

## Level 4: Practical Failure Modes

### How could someone game this score?

1. **Add empty files.** Each new file increases potential for unused, logs, structural, smells without adding any findings. Score goes up.

2. **Split files instead of fixing them.** A 600-LOC file flagged as structural gets split into two 300-LOC files. Both disappear from the structural detector. One finding removed, one potential added. Score goes up. The code may or may not be better.

3. **Use barrel files aggressively.** Barrel re-exports increase the exports denominator without adding real API surface. Score goes up.

4. **Avoid deprecated annotations.** If you never mark things `@deprecated`, the deprecated detector has zero potential, zero findings. Dimension shrinks. You're rewarded for not documenting deprecation.

5. **Wontfix everything (lenient mode).** The lenient score only counts "open" as failures. Marking everything wontfix makes the score go to 100%. The strict score catches this, but the headline `objective_score` is the lenient one.

6. **Run without slow phases.** Skip the dupes phase (slow=True). Entire dimension disappears. Score is computed without it. No asterisk on the number.

### Goodhart's Law risks

The most likely Goodhart failure mode: **optimizing for the score leads to architectural homogenization.** If the tool penalizes large files, complexity, and structural signals, developers will aggressively split files to stay under thresholds. This can produce a codebase of many tiny files that are individually "clean" but collectively incoherent -- the complexity has been pushed from within files to between files (more imports, more indirection, more files to navigate). The tool checks for this somewhat (single_use, coupling), but not enough to counterbalance the pressure.

Another risk: **the score anchors expectations.** Once a team sees 95.0, they expect monotonic improvement. But legitimate refactoring (extracting a shared library, reorganizing modules) can temporarily drop the score. If the score is in a dashboard or CI check, this creates pressure to avoid structural improvements that would trigger temporary regressions.

### What real quality problems would this score miss entirely?

1. **Semantic correctness.** The tool cannot detect wrong business logic, incorrect state machines, race conditions, or data integrity bugs. This is expected but worth stating.

2. **Insufficient abstraction.** If 10 files each implement similar logic slightly differently (but not similar enough for the dupe detector), the tool won't flag it. The absence of a shared abstraction is invisible.

3. **Over-abstraction.** The tool flags large files and god components but has no detector for premature or excessive abstraction -- deep inheritance trees, DI containers that obscure control flow, utility functions that abstract nothing.

4. **Test quality.** No dimension for test coverage, test clarity, or test maintenance burden.

5. **Type safety.** No detector for `any` usage, unsafe casts, or type assertion abuse in TypeScript.

6. **Documentation debt.** No detector for undocumented public APIs, misleading comments, or stale documentation.

7. **Performance.** No detector for n+1 queries, unnecessary re-renders, unbounded list renders, or memory leaks.

### When would the score go UP while the code gets WORSE?

1. **Adding boilerplate.** Auto-generating a bunch of clean-but-useless files (types, constants, utils stubs) inflates denominators. Score goes up.

2. **Deleting a problematic module entirely** (giving up on functionality) removes both findings and potentials, but if the module had disproportionate findings, the score improves.

3. **Inlining shared code.** If you copy a shared utility into each consumer (eliminating the single-use finding), the score improves, but you now have duplication that might not be caught (especially if slow=True skips the dupe phase).

4. **Splitting a moderately complex file into two trivially simple files.** The complexity was real and necessary; splitting it doesn't reduce inherent complexity, it just distributes it. But the structural detector no longer flags either file.

### When would the score go DOWN while the code gets BETTER?

1. **Deleting dead code.** Clean files that contributed to denominators are removed. Score drops.

2. **Adding `@deprecated` annotations.** You're being honest about API lifecycle, but now the deprecated detector has more potential and possibly more findings.

3. **Consolidating small files into a well-structured larger file.** File count drops (denominators shrink), and the consolidated file might trigger structural/complexity detectors.

4. **Adding a new detector to the tool.** More detectors = more findings on the same codebase = lower score. This is a tool-versioning problem: the score is not comparable across tool versions.

5. **Running the slow dupes phase for the first time.** A new dimension appears with findings. Score drops. This is especially insidious because the previous "95.0" was computed without this dimension, but there's no flag indicating incompleteness.

---

## Level 5: What Would You Change?

### Critical fixes (minimum viable)

**1. Fix the inflated denominators.**

The cycles and coupling denominators are the most egregious. Cycles potential should be the number of strongly-connected components of size >= 2 in the dependency graph, or the number of files that participate in any cycle, or simply the number of modules -- not the total edge count. Coupling potential should be the number of import edges that cross architectural boundaries, not all edges.

For exports: the denominator should be non-trivially-exported symbols (exclude re-exports from barrels) or, more conservatively, just use file count.

**2. Add a completeness indicator.**

The score should report which phases were actually run. If dupes was skipped (slow=True), the score should either (a) not be reported, (b) be reported with an asterisk, or (c) include a "completeness: 7/8 phases" metric. Running 7/8 phases and calling the result a definitive score is misleading.

**3. Cap minimum potential or add small-sample warnings.**

Pattern consistency with 42 checks should not contribute equally to a dimension that has 3942 checks. Options: (a) require minimum potential of 100 to include a dimension, (b) Bayesian shrinkage toward 100% for small samples, (c) report confidence intervals alongside point estimates.

The simplest approach: multiply the dimension's weight by `min(1.0, potential / 200)`, dampening the influence of small-sample dimensions.

**4. Prevent score-goes-up-when-you-add-clean-files.**

This requires changing the formula from a ratio to something that accounts for absolute issue counts. One approach: score = f(weighted_failures) rather than f(weighted_failures / potential). But this loses the normalization that makes scores comparable across codebases.

A middle ground: use potential from the *first* scan as a fixed baseline, and only update it when detectors change. Then adding files doesn't inflate the denominator. But this introduces statefulness and baseline drift issues.

Another approach: track the score over time and flag "score increased without any findings being resolved" as a suspicious signal.

### Structural improvements (ideal system)

**5. Separate severity from detection confidence.**

Currently, confidence serves double duty. A finding should have:
- `confidence`: how sure are we this is a real issue? (affects whether it's worth looking at)
- `severity`: if it is real, how bad is it? (affects how much it should weigh on the score)

A low-confidence, high-severity finding (possible god component) should be treated differently from a high-confidence, low-severity finding (definite naming inconsistency).

**6. Use issue-count-based scoring within dimensions, not pass-rate.**

Instead of `(potential - failures) / potential`, consider a diminishing-returns model: the first N issues in a dimension are serious, subsequent ones have decreasing marginal impact. This could be modeled as `score = 100 * exp(-k * weighted_failures / sqrt(potential))` or similar.

This would mean: Component design with 97 issues is genuinely bad and should score low, regardless of whether there are 447 or 4470 checkable things. The current ratio-based approach masks severity when denominators are large.

**7. Weight dimensions by information content, not just tier.**

A dimension where the detector found zero issues (orphaned, naming) contributes a perfect 100% to the score. This is not information. It's weight given to "we checked and found nothing." If a dimension is always 100%, it should gradually lose influence in the overall score (or be folded into another dimension).

Conversely, a dimension with high variance across scans is more informative and should retain its weight.

**8. Track delta, not just absolute.**

The most useful number is not "95.0" but "+1.2 since last scan" or "-0.3 from fixing 5 issues and removing 20 clean files." The absolute score is context-free; the delta tells a story. The state system already has scan history infrastructure (`scan_count`, `last_scan`) but doesn't compute deltas.

**9. Address the smells unit mismatch.**

The smells detector potential is "files" (958), but findings are per (file, smell_id). A file with 5 smells generates 5 weighted failures against a potential contribution of 1. This means a single very smelly file could contribute 5.0 weighted failures to a denominator of 1, yielding a negative unclamped pass rate. The `max(0.0, ...)` clamp silently hides this. 

Fix: either make potential = total (file, smell) combinations checked, or cap per-file weighted failures at 1.0.

**10. Consider non-linear tier weighting.**

The current linear {1:1, 2:2, 3:3, 4:4} underweights the importance difference between tiers. If a tier-4 issue (import cycle) is truly more severe than a tier-1 issue (unused import), the weight ratio should probably be more like 1:2:5:10 than 1:2:3:4. The current weights produce a score that is 69% determined by tier-3 dimensions simply because most dimensions are tier-3. This makes the tier system almost decorative.

### Summary

The scoring system has a coherent structure and reasonable ambitions. Its core weakness is that **it conflates "fraction of checks that passed" with "code health,"** and this conflation is compounded by inconsistent and sometimes badly inflated denominators. The worst offenders are the cycles (3942 edges) and coupling (2711 edges) detectors, whose enormous denominators guarantee near-perfect scores that pull the overall number up regardless of real quality. The missing dupes dimension, the volatile 42-check patterns dimension, and the unit mismatch in smells are secondary but real problems.

The most important single fix is to make denominators semantically honest: potential should be the number of things that *could reasonably fail this check*, not the number of things that were *in scope when the check ran*. The distinction between "958 files could have unused imports" (correct) and "3942 dependency edges could be import cycles" (meaningfully wrong) is the difference between a metric that means something and one that flatters.</result>

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   The user has several interleaved requests:
   - **Implement all 5 scoring fixes** from the deep critique synthesis: (1) fix cycle/coupling denominators, (2) dampen small-sample dimensions, (3) add completeness indicator, (4) fix smells unit mismatch, (5) show dimension breakdown prominently — ALL COMPLETED
   - **Always show dual scores** (health + strict) for both overall AND per-dimension — "can we have strict and overall score for each denominator too?" — COMPLETED
   - **Implement PR #5** into the code — exclusion matching, dep graph, module regex fixes + directory move support — COMPLETED (manually applied since architectures diverged)
   - **Push EVERYTHING to main** — NOT YET DONE
   - **Check latest GitHub issue (#6)** and ask about folder support — COMPLETED, user chose "Files + directories"
   - **Implement Issue #6** (re-export facade detector for files AND directories) — IMPLEMENTED, was adding to DETECTOR_NAMES when interrupted

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - **Dimension-based scoring**: 11 dimensions, tier-weighted (T1-T4), confidence-weighted
   - **Sample-size dampening**: `MIN_SAMPLE = 200`, weight = `tier_weight * min(1.0, checks/200)` — prevents small-sample dimensions from swinging overall score
   - **Per-file cap for smells**: `_FILE_BASED_DETECTORS = {"smells"}` — when potential = files but findings are per-(file, sub-type), cap per-file weighted failures at 1.0
   - **Cycle denominator fix**: Changed from `total_edges` (inflated, 3942 for reigh) to `len(graph)` (total files in dep graph)
   - **Scan completeness tracking**: `state["scan_completeness"][lang] = "full"|"fast"` based on whether slow phases were included
   - **Re-export facade detection**: Files that are pure re-export (only import/from-import statements + docstrings + `__all__`), with <=2 importers; also directory-level facades where all modules in a package are facades
   - **Editable pip install**: `/private/tmp/desloppify/` is the correct source; `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/` is the user's separate working copy, NOT the right one
   - **PR #5 changes**: Exclusion matching uses relative paths (avoids matching project root name), Python dep graph regex matches absolute from-imports, module word boundary uses lookaround instead of `\b`, directory move support added

Good question. The facade detector is generic (shared `detectors/facade.py`), but it needs to be registered in both Python and TypeScript because each language has its own:
1. **DETECTOR_NAMES** — the list of valid names for `desloppify detect <name>`
2. **detect_commands** — the function to run when `desloppify detect facade` is called (each needs its own dep graph builder + language flag)
3. **phase runners** — how the detector integrates into scan (each language's coupling phase calls it differently)

This is actually correct — the detector itself is shared code, but the *wiring* is per-language because:
- Python builds its dep graph from `from .detectors.deps import build_dep_graph` (Python import parser)
- TypeScript builds its dep graph from its own `build_dep_graph` (TS import parser)
- The detect command passes `lang="python"` vs `lang="typescript"` to the facade detector

So the *logic* is in one place (`detectors/facade.py`), but each language registers a thin wrapper command that passes its own graph builder and language flag. Same pattern as `orphaned`, `cycles`, `single-use` — all shared detectors with per-language wiring.

That said, `DETECTOR_NAMES` could be auto-derived from the `detect_commands` dict keys, which would eliminate one duplication. Let me do that quick improvement.

> AGENT

Everything is pushed and PR #5 is closed.

About the `--lang python` error — that's because `--lang` is a **global** flag that must come before the subcommand, not after it. The correct syntax is:

```
desloppify --lang python scan --skip-slow
```

Not:
```
desloppify scan --lang python --skip-slow   # ← wrong: --lang is a global arg
```

This is how argparse works with subcommands — global arguments (`--lang`, `--exclude`) go before the subcommand name, subcommand-specific arguments (`--skip-slow`, `--json`, `--top`) go after. I just used the wrong order in my test command. The tool itself is working correctly — you can see the successful scan right after where I used the right syntax.

**Summary of everything pushed to main:**

1. **Scoring fixes** — cycle denominator (files not edges), small-sample dampening (MIN_SAMPLE=200), smells per-file cap, scan completeness tracking
2. **Dual scores everywhere** — Health + Strict shown for overall and per-dimension in status, scan deltas, plan markdown, and next item context
3. **Facade detector** (Issue #6) — detects re-export facade files and directories for both Python and TypeScript
4. **PR #5 fixes** — exclusion matching, Python dep graph absolute from-imports, module regex word boundary, directory move support
5. **Detector registration cleanup** — removed redundant `DETECTOR_NAMES` lists, now derived from `detect_commands` dict keys (one place to register = works everywhere)

> DEVELOPER

You left this comment on that other issue: Thanks for the detailed writeup. This is a real and important issue.

Changes made:

Updated broad_except label to include guidance: "Broad except — check library exceptions before narrowing". This reminds developers (and AI agents) that narrowing requires understanding what exceptions dependencies raise.

Severity was already "medium" (not "high"), which is appropriate — except Exception is a code smell worth flagging but not always wrong.

Why not add a complementary detector (Option A):

A narrow_except_risk detector that checks for external library calls in try blocks would be valuable but is hard to do accurately — it would need to resolve imports, distinguish stdlib from third-party, and know which calls can raise. False positives from this detector could be worse than the current guidance gap.

The fundamental insight from this issue is correct: the tool should guide toward informed narrowing, not just any narrowing. The updated label communicates this at the point of detection.

Re: other observations:

Good points about magic number over-extraction and over-decomposition. These are judgment calls that the tool can't make for you — but the severity/tier system tries to signal which findings are high-value (T1/T2) vs "consider this" (T3/T4). Magic numbers are already "low" severity.
